---
shortcode: vedyarlng
name: {full: Vedyari Language, aliases: []}
type: skill
subType: language
description: "The language of the Vedyara city-states—a family in its own right, whose Classical register stands apart from every spoken vernacular."
tags: []
data:
  roman: "abcdefghijklmnoprstuvyáéíóúë0123456789-'"
  core: true
  abbreviation: Ved.
  fields:
    - name: "Sun, moon, sky and the reckoning of time"
      holds: "The sun, the moon and the sky, the day, the month, the season and the year, and the names of the calendar."
    - name: "Water, rain and the monsoon"
      holds: "Rivers, seas and wells, rain, cloud and storm, flood and drought, boats and landings."
    - name: "Mountain, forest, field and the shapes of the land"
      holds: "Mountains, peaks, forests, plains and deserts, the ground a village stands on, and the words that name a place."
    - name: "Stone, metal and treasure"
      holds: "Rock, gold, copper, iron and silver, jewels and salt."
    - name: "Crops, beasts and growing things"
      holds: "Grain, cane, cotton and the other crops, the herds, the beasts of the road and of the forest, flowers and trees."
    - name: "Kin, household and the person"
      holds: "The household and its kin, marriage, the body and the qualities a name wishes on a child."
    - name: "Station, rank and office"
      holds: "The four stations, standing within them, the assemblies, and the titles of the men and women who hold office."
    - name: "Gods, rites and the temple"
      holds: "The gods, the schools of the faith, the temple and its priests, vows, offerings, dreams and fire."
    - name: "Death, the dead and the ancestors"
      holds: "Death and the dead, the burning, mourning and the ancestors."
    - name: "War, arms and the host"
      holds: "The host and its captains, the bow and the other arms, the fort, the elephant and the battle."
    - name: "Craft, trade and the counting-house"
      holds: "Weaving, dyeing and the other crafts, the market and the caravan, coin, debt and the ledger."
    - name: "Duty, law and judgment"
      holds: "Duty and the law, the court and the judgment, honor, shame, oath and forgiveness."
    - name: "Speech, learning and the written word"
      holds: "Speech and song, the recitation, writing, the script, memory and learning."
    - name: "Doing, going and being"
      holds: "The verbs of everyday speech and the deeds an epic tells."
    - name: Numbers and grammar words
      holds: "Numbers, pronouns, pointing words and the particles that hold a sentence together."
  affixes:
    - form: "-ija"
      gloss: "one born to it: the names of the four stations"
      attaches: "replaces a closing vowel"
      replaces: vowel
      examples: ["karmu > karmija"]
    - form: "-oka"
      gloss: "one who does it"
      attaches: "added to a verb stem"
      examples: ["gan- > ganoka"]
    - form: "-ona"
      gloss: "the doing of it"
      attaches: "added to a verb stem"
      examples: ["dah- > dahona"]
    - form: "-uta"
      gloss: "one to whom it is done"
      attaches: "added to a verb stem"
      examples: ["pat- > patuta"]
    - form: "-ru"
      gloss: "done, finished"
      attaches: "added to a verb stem"
      examples: ["muk- > mukru"]
    - form: "-in"
      gloss: "one who has or keeps it"
      attaches: "replaces a closing short a"
      replaces: "a"
      examples: ["vreta > vretin"]
    - form: "-ina"
      gloss: "one of the kind, of good standing in it"
      attaches: "replaces a closing short a"
      replaces: "a"
    - form: "-i"
      gloss: "a woman's form of a word; one who deals in it"
      attaches: "replaces a closing short a"
      replaces: "a"
      examples: ["sundora > sundeli"]
    - form: "-ika"
      gloss: "a woman's name"
      attaches: "replaces a closing short a"
      replaces: "a"
    - form: "-i"
      gloss: "one from it, said of a place or a land"
      attaches: "replaces a closing short a"
      replaces: "a"
      examples: ["vedyara > vedyari"]
    - form: "-an"
      gloss: "belonging to it: a god's, a land's"
      attaches: "replaces a closing short a"
      replaces: "a"
      examples: ["vedyara > vedyaran"]
    - form: "-ka"
      gloss: "one who works it, or a thing of it"
      attaches: "added"
      examples: ["lipu > lepika"]
    - {form: "-ni", gloss: "the leader of it", attaches: "added", examples: ["senta > senoni"]}
    - {form: "-da", gloss: "that gives it", attaches: "added", examples: ["sartu > sarveda"]}
    - form: "-s"
      gloss: "more than one, as these pages write the plural"
      attaches: "added"
      examples: ["svapnadeva > svapnadevas"]
    - {form: -i, gloss: a woman's name only, kind: name, given: woman}
    - {form: -ika, gloss: a woman's name only, kind: name, given: woman}
    - {form: -ika, gloss: a woman's name only, kind: name, given: woman}
    - {form: -a, gloss: a woman's name only, kind: name, given: woman}
    - {form: -a, gloss: the names of both men and women, kind: name, given: either}
    - {form: -i, gloss: the names of both men and women, kind: name, given: either}
    - {form: -n, gloss: "the names of both, a man's more often", kind: name, given: either}
    - {form: -r, gloss: "the names of both, a man's more often", kind: name, given: either}
    - {form: -t, gloss: "the names of both, a man's more often", kind: name, given: either}

    - {form: -m, gloss: "the names of both, a man's more often", kind: name, given: either}
    - {form: -d, gloss: "the names of both, a man's more often", kind: name, given: either}

  sounds:
    edges:
      - edge: openCluster
        sounds: [pr, tr, dr, kr, gr, br, bhr, vr, shr, sm, st, sv, dv, vy, ny, jv, jn]
      - {edge: closeConsonant, sounds: [n, r, t, k, s, l, m, d]}
    clusters: {max: 3}
    joining:
      - {first: [a], second: [a], written: a, example: _podma_ + _avoli_ = _padmavali_}
      - {first: [a], second: [i], written: e, example: _vyela_ + _ishvora_ = _vyaleshvora_}
      - {first: [a], second: [u], written: o}
      - first: [i]
        second: [a, u, e, o]
        written: y+
        example: "_nadri_ + _avoli_ = _nadryavoli_, a _y_ between"
      - {first: [u], second: [a, i, e, o], written: v+}
  elements:
    - {form: pur, position: closing, kind: place, sense: a town, examples: [rajepur]}
    - {form: grima, position: closing, kind: place, sense: a village, examples: [lipigram]}
    - {form: kotra, position: closing, kind: place, sense: a fort, examples: [danurkot]}
    - {form: girka, position: closing, kind: place, sense: a mountain, examples: [suvargeri]}
    - {form: shikhena, position: closing, kind: place, sense: a peak, examples: [suryashikhar]}
    - form: dvarna
      position: closing
      kind: place
      sense: a pass through the wall
      examples: [shunyadvar]
    - form: vanta
      position: closing
      kind: place
      sense: a forest or a grove
      examples: [veniban]
    - form: shetka
      position: closing
      kind: place
      sense: a field country
      examples: [ikshukshetra]
    - form: stalu
      position: closing
      kind: place
      sense: a stretch of ground
      examples: [vrihistal]
    - form: padsa
      position: closing
      kind: place
      sense: land at the foot of something
      examples: [nadipada]
    - {form: tirsa, position: closing, kind: place, sense: a bank or a shore, examples: [nilatir]}
    - {form: ghatka, position: closing, kind: place, sense: a landing, examples: [naughat]}
    - {form: kupra, position: closing, kind: place, sense: a well, examples: [marukupa]}
    - form: mukhva
      position: closing
      kind: place
      sense: "a mouth, of a river or a pass"
      examples: [chandramukha]
    - form: prabhana
      position: closing
      kind: place
      sense: "a source, a glacier-spring"
      examples: [mahaprabhava]
    - {form: derka, position: closing, kind: place, sense: a stream, examples: [hiranyadara]}
    - {form: samedra, position: closing, kind: place, sense: a sea, examples: [meghra-samedra]}
  callingNames:
    minSyllables: 4
    closings:
      - {form: -u, usual: man}
      - {form: -i, usual: woman}
      - {form: -a, usual: either}
      - {doubled: true, usual: either}
  tongues:
    - {tag: vedyari, meaning: a Vedyari name}
    - {tag: translation, meaning: a name given in the language of these pages}
    - {tag: exonym:<tongue>, meaning: "a name another people gives, in the tongue named"}
    - tag: other:<tongue>
      meaning: "a name in another tongue of the setting, as its speakers give it"
    - tag: calling
      meaning: "a calling name, cut from the given name its **Built from** cell gives"
  register:
    - {name: Vedyara Adventurer's Guide, tongue: translation}
    - {name: ganoka-shalda, tongue: vedyari, built: [ganoka, shalda]}
    - {name: The Reckoners' College, tongue: translation}
    - {name: Struck from the Roll, tongue: translation}
    - {name: Hearer, tongue: translation}
    - {name: Slate-holder, tongue: translation}
    - {name: ganoka, tongue: vedyari, built: [ganoka]}
    - {name: Reckoner of the Hall, tongue: translation}
    - {name: Keeper of the Tables, tongue: translation}
    - {name: Court Ganoka, tongue: translation}
    - {name: Reckoner of the Weighing, tongue: translation}
    - {name: Tide-reader, tongue: translation}
    - {name: The Threshold-keepers, tongue: translation}
    - {name: The Keepers of the Threshold, tongue: translation}

# terran_analog: Vedyari reads Indian. Its words and names take the sound of Sanskrit and Hindi and keep clear of the whole words, and the joining rules are Sanskrit vowel sandhi; the corpus relies on that resemblance for common nouns, titles and offices. It keeps clear of the Sanskrit names for the four stations (Karmija, Danhija, Sentija, Ritrija), for the word for station (tarona), and for the gods, whose names are coined. The coined roots in this lexicon (every row attested `—`) are made to the rules and are not the Sanskrit, Hindi or Tamil word for their meaning.
---

Vedyari is a tongue of the Vedyari (isolate) family. Fluency measures the sophistication of expression in Vedyari, from the halting phrases of a traveler to the nuanced and learned discourse of a native speaker. As with all specific languages, this skill inherits its mechanics from the general [[sohl-none-docskill-lang|Language]] skill.

## Overview

Vedyari is the primary tongue of the city-states of [[place-vedyarargn|Vedyara Region]]. It is a language of considerable antiquity, and it has parted across the centuries into a classical and a vernacular form. Classical Vedyari serves the sacred texts, the legal documents and the formal ceremonies, and differs markedly from the spoken dialects of common discourse. It is a weighty and complex tongue, and the civilization that speaks it prizes precision, ritual and philosophical inquiry.

Vedyari speakers are found throughout the city-states and in trading communities across the eastern world. The language has proven remarkably durable, maintaining grammatical coherence even as phonological shifts have created regional variation.

## How Vedyari sounds

Vedyari has two registers, and they sound alike but end differently. The classical tongue of the temple, the court and the law ends its words on a vowel. The tongue of the road, the market and the house closes many words on a consonant. Most words have two or three syllables, a name is usually two words joined, and a word of four syllables is long.

**The vowels.** _a_ carries most words, with _i_ and _u_ after it. _e_ and _o_ are rarer, and few words open on them. Two vowels stand together only as _ai_ and _au_.

**The consonants.** _r_ and _n_ lead. The plain stops _t_, _k_, _d_ and _p_ come next, then _s_, _m_ and _v_, with _sh_ and _j_ for colour. _bh_ is common, as in _bharava_ and _bhurka_: a _b_ said with a breath after it, the two letters as the guide reads them, said together. Vedyari writes _bh_, as the guide allows a tongue whose note says so. _kh_ and _gh_ keep the guide's throat sounds and belong to a smaller, older set of words, such as _ghaul_ "night" and _okhra_ "a loom"; most of them stand at the start of a syllable. A new word reaches for a stop before a _v_ or an _r_.

**Clusters.** Consonants meet in a few favoured pairs and triples inside a word: _ndr_, _shr_, _kr_, _dr_, _vr_ and _pr_. A word opens on at most two consonants together. _ksh_ stands inside a word, as in _akshora_, never at its start.

**Endings.** A classical word ends on a vowel, most often _-a_: the gods _Rasikara_ and _Kalavrata_, the titles _maharava_ and _senapavi_, the words _dorma_ "duty" and _janpoda_. A person or a thing done ends in _-ona_, _-ika_, _-in_, _-ini_ or _-ija_, and a lord or a god in _-endra_, _-esha_ or _-deva_. The spoken word may close on one consonant, _n_, _r_, _t_, _k_, _s_, _l_, _m_ or _d_, or on _nd_: _lam_ "a child", _ghal_ "a sword", _daum_ "eight". A place people travel to closes the same way: a town in _-pur_, a village in _-gram_, a landing in _-ghat_, a fort in _-kot_, a pass in _-dvar_, a peak in _-shikhar_, a grove in _-ban_, a ground in _-stal_, a seat in _-stan_, a bank in _-tir_, and a keeper's land in _-pal_; a hero's name ends in _-bir_ or _-bira_; a weekday ends in _-var_. A given name ends on _-a_, _-i_ or a consonant (_Chandreli_, _Shilabir_, _Jayanit_); a clan name more often on a vowel.

**Built names.** A name is one word or two joined, seldom three: _Chandreli_ is _chendra_ and _sundeli_, "moon, the beautiful", and _Danurkirti_ is "bow-fame". A name of five syllables or more is an old name or a title.

**What Vedyari leaves out.** It has no _th_ and no _dh_, since the guide reads both as the sounds of _thin_ and _this_, and no _ph_; the sound of _f_ is written _f_. It avoids _lv_ and the endings of [[skill-helonclng|Helonic Language]], _-os_ and _-is_. No word closes on two consonants but _nd_, and none closes on _sh_, _kh_, _bh_, _v_ or _h_.

## Phonology

### Consonant System

Vedyari employs a rich consonantal inventory marked by articulatory precision:

**Stops and Affricates:**

Unvoiced
: p, t, k

Voiced
: b, d, g

Affricates
: ch, j, ts

Breathed
: bh, a _b_ with a breath after it

**Fricatives:** s, sh, h, f in a few words, and the throat sounds kh and gh

**Nasals:** m, n, ny, and ng inside a word

**Liquids & Approximants:** l, r; y, v

**Distinctive Features:**

- A few words double a consonant, said once and held a little (_uttarka_, _Pallovi_)

### Vowel System

Vedyari distinguishes five cardinal vowels:

**Vowels:** a, i, u, e (front), o (back)

**Diphthongs:** Common sequences include ai, au, which may function as monophthongs in rapid speech

**Vowel Harmony Patterns:** Historically significant; elements that select for front vowels (i, e) or back vowels (a, o, u) tend to cluster within morphemes.

### Phonotactic Patterns

Analysis of attested names reveals:

Consonant Clusters
: A word opens on a single consonant or on one of the pairs in the word-edges table. Inside a word no more than three consonants stand together, as in _Chendra_ and _Sandva_. _ksh_ stands only inside a word. The aspirates _kh_, _gh_ and _bh_ are single consonants, so _Bharava_ opens on one

Syllable Structure
: Predominantly CV or CVC; onset-heavy structure favors initial consonants

Final Consonants
: A word closes on a vowel or on _n_, _r_, _t_, _k_, _s_, _l_, _m_ or _d_, or on _nd_

Word Length
: Most words and names have two or three syllables. A name is one word or two joined, and a name of five syllables or more is an old name or a title.

## Grammar Notes

### Word Order

Classical Vedyari follows a **Subject-Object-Verb (SOV)** order in main clauses, though marked inversions occur in poetry and philosophical verse.

### Nominal System

- **Nouns** have number (singular/plural) and, arguably, honorific status
- **Agreement:** Adjectives and determiners agree with nouns in gender (masculine, feminine, neuter)
- **Compounds:** Noun-noun and adjective-noun compounds are extremely productive; the rightmost element bears primary stress

### Participles

- Participial forms are common as attributive modifiers and in periphrastic constructions

## Everyday words and expressions

A sight, _darsika_, is what is seen; _dirmu_ is how a person or thing looks. _Sarnu_ is a bodily sensation. _Shardun_ is regret, and _dormabrak_ is the distress of guilt over one's own wrongdoing.

_Kamva_ is desire and love in the broad sense. _Nertakamva_ is romantic love, _narmakamva_ love between friends, _etkakamva_ love of kin, and _sartukamva_ selfless love extended to all. Sexual lust is _kramu_. A longing for someone absent is _kamvanyur_; _purkukamva_ is the feeling of satisfaction when a desire is fulfilled. The settled ease of contentment is _nishta_.

_Sotya_ is truth; _sotyanyur_ is a person's certainty that a claim is true, which may be mistaken. _Drinnu_ is confidence in one's own ability or prospects. _Vremu_ is trust in another; _vrenu_ is faith without complete proof.

The ordinary spoken verbs _darka_ “see”, _bhinu_ “hear” and _ushta_ “hold” are related words beside the bound citations _darsh-_, _bhin-_ and _usht-_. Each is a free word in its own right. _Mo surta darka_ means “I see the sun”; _mo ravha bhinu_, “I hear a voice”; and _mo jalka ushta_, “I hold water”. These examples use the subject first, then the object and the action. The old formal _ushtami_ remains in the devotion formula below.

_Mo virmu virna_ is “I smell a scent”; _mo talma talnu_, “I taste a flavour”; _mo pomba partu_, “I touch a hand”; and _mo sarnu sarpu_, “I feel a bodily sensation”. _Mo surta darmu_ means “I look at the sun”. With _nyura_, sustained attention, _mo surta nyura darmu_ is “I watch the sun”, and _mo ravha nyura bhinu_ is “I listen to a voice”. _Mo tirma smirdak_ is “I recognize the name”. _Ehu sundora dirma_ means “This seems beautiful”.

For an emotional feeling, _mo tranka ushta_ means “I feel anger”, literally “I hold anger”; _mo anonda ushta_ is “I feel joy”, and _mo shardun ushta_ is “I feel regret”. These are ordinary feeling expressions. They do not describe bodily sensation, for which _sarpu_ is used.

A spoken quality statement can use _orsa_ “be”: _mo ghomrin orsa_, “I am hungry”; _mo jaltruk orsa_, “I am thirsty”; and _mo nivre orsa_, “I am awake”. _Mo ghomra ghuma_ is “I eat food”, and _mo jalpun jalnu_, “I drink a drink”. _Mo nidu_ is “I sleep”; _mo nivra_, “I wake”. These are the particular spoken forms used here; the bound historical citations remain as printed in the dictionary.

_Mo rinu nimar_ means “I inhale”, drawing air inward, and _mo turu nimar_ means “I exhale”, letting it outward. _Nimru_ is breathable air, distinct from _pavanda_, wind. When breathing is blocked or no breathable air is available, _mo nimbrak_ means “I am suffocating”. Ordinary exertion or breathlessness alone is not the meaning of this expression.

_Mo nu shermak_ means “I hurt you”, causing pain; _mo shermu sarpu_ means “I hurt”, experiencing bodily pain. _Mo petsa petsak_ is “I heal a wound”, while _mo kalnun_ is “I recover”, regaining health. _Mo puratra barden_ means “I grow older”; this names the process, while _puratra_ alone is the quality “old”.

_Mo lamnyur ushta_ means “I am pregnant”, carrying a developing child in the womb. In a birth account, _mo lam pomu_ means “I give birth to a child”. _Mo lam tarmu_ means “I nurse a child”, feeding the child at the breast. _Bhonru_ is the outside of the belly, _ghomrakoshta_ the stomach within it, and _lamkoshta_ the womb. _Visherta_ is a testicle, distinct from _uttarka perta_, the thigh.

_Tobri_ is either parent and _bhaulku_ either sibling; these words let _tobribhaul_ and _tobribhauli_ name an uncle and aunt on either side of the family, and _bhaulkukema_ and _bhaulkukemri_ a nephew and niece through either sibling. _Sutka_ already names a line of descent; _kulpa_ keeps its narrower meaning of kin within a station. _Kobala_ is the household family.

_Agmistalu_ is the physical hearth, while _agmi_ is its fire. _Lerkaralmu_ is the wool of a sheep's fleece; _ralmu_ includes animal hair, not only the hair of a person's head. The tree-based words _ferkachel_ “leaf” and _ferkaghomra_ “fruit” are used for other plants too. _Meghruvas_ names cloudless weather; _meghranda_ describes a cloudy sky.

After eating food or drinking, _mo purku orsa_ means “I am sated”, full after the meal or drink. _Purku_ keeps its ordinary sense “full”; this particular expression gives its bodily use.

A numbered position is a _ganstal_. _Ganstal aisu_, _ganstal birsa_ and _ganstal tre_ name first, second and third. Further positions use their number in the same expression: _ganstal sotra_ is fourth, _ganstal netu_ seventh, and _ganstal teska_ tenth. _Khondra ro birsa_ is a half, one of two equal shares of a whole; _khondra ro sotra_ is a quarter, one of four equal shares. _Sartu birsa_ takes both of a pair; _aisu ro birsa_ permits either member of that pair, while _uvas aisu ro birsa_ excludes both alternatives.

For a comparison, _ehu uhu vrun bram drau orsa_ means “This is longer than that”. _Vrun_ marks the thing compared against. _Ehu sartu navu vrun bram drau orsa_ means “This is the longest of them”, longer than all the others in the group being compared. With _nyem_ instead of _bram_, the same stated comparison gives the shortest. _Ehu uhu shem samru orsa_ means “This is equal to that”; equality in size or amount does not make two things _samnu_, identical.

_Mo koda shem hauvu_ means “I bring a pot”, coming with the pot. _Mo groha rinu chambu_ is “I enter the house”, and _mo groha turu chambu_ is “I leave the house”. _Mo nu anva chambu_ means “I follow you”. _Mo pertajortu pa sadnu_ is “I kneel”, resting on my knees. _Mo koda utrak tarpu_ means “I lift the pot”, raising it upward. _Mo ehu darsin karmak_ is “I reveal this”, making the hidden thing visible.

_Mo bravul lampu_ means “I shout”; _mo nimral lampu_, “I whisper”. The first uses a strong voice, the second a soft breathy utterance without a voiced murmur. _Mo pratnu lampu_ is “I express thanks”, expressing gratitude. _Mo vreta pomu_ means “I promise”, giving my pledged word. In a purchase, _mo orgha pomu_ means “I buy”, paying the price for the goods; in a sale, _mo orgha adnu_ means “I sell”, receiving that price. _Mo khondra pomu_ means “I share”, giving another a portion.

_Mo groha trum chambu_ means “I go from the house”; _mo groha tral chambu_ is “I go to the house”. Here _trum_ gives the source and _tral_ the destination. _Mo groha bhe orsa_ means “I am at home”; _mo groha rinru orsa_ places me inside it, and _mo groha turam orsa_ outside it. _Mo dvarna anshe orsa_ is “I am beside the door”. _Mo ehu le uhu minra orsa_ puts me between the two things indicated; _mo janra minra orsa_ puts me among the people.

_Mo ro koda_ is “my pot”: _ro_ links the possessor _mo_ to _koda_. _Nu ro koda_ is “your pot”, and _mova ro groha_ “our house”. _Ehu mo ro orsa_ means “This belongs to me”; _ehu nu ro orsa_ means “This is yours”; and _ehu mova ro orsa_ means “This is ours”. _Mo brel darka_ means “I see myself”; _ve brel darka_ means “He or she sees himself or herself”. In each of these clauses, _brel_ refers back to that clause's subject. _Ve mo darka_ instead means “He or she sees me”.

_Lamprabhana ro kalda bhe mo groha bhe orsa_ means “During the birth I am at home”. The named event _lamprabhana_ stands before _ro kalda bhe_, “at the time of”. _Lamprabhana ro kalda trum mo groha bhe orsa_ means “Since the birth I am at home”. _Mo lamprabhana grun nirpak_ means “I wait until the birth”. _Mo koda ushta kaldem nu lampu_ means “I hold the pot while you speak”. These examples retain the particular spoken verb forms already cited.

_Aisu demrak bhe mo ehu darka_ means “I see this once”, on one occasion; _birsa demrak bhe mo ehu darka_ means “I see this twice”, on two occasions. _Demrak_ counts occasions, while _vramul_ means “again”; _vramul vramul_ is “again and again”. _Mo prenu ghuma_ means “I have already eaten”, with _prenu_ locating the eating before the time in question. _Mo uvas ghuma renuk_ means “I have not eaten yet”; _mo nyuru nirpak_ means “I am still waiting”. In these particular utterances, the separate time words supply the time being described.

The question words fit complete spoken questions. _Jahu surta darka?_ asks “Who sees the sun?”, and _nu kevu darka?_ asks “What do you see?”. _Nu kren kertuk?_ asks “Which one do you choose?”. _Nu kevu stalu bhe orsa?_ asks “Where are you?”, and _nu kevu kalda bhe hauvu?_ “When do you come?”. _Nu kevu karun drun chambu?_ asks “Why do you go?”, the reason for going; _nu kevu prabru druk chambu?_ asks “For what purpose do you go?”, the intended aim. _Nu dvarna kevu pantra shem muku?_ asks “How do you open the door?”, by what means.

_Nu kevu ganru koda ushta?_ asks “How many pots do you have?”; the number is wanted. _Nu kevu ganmul jalka ushta?_ asks “How much water do you have?”; the amount is wanted. _Ehu jahu ro koda orsa?_ asks “Whose pot is this?”. _Nu koda jahu tral pomu?_ asks “To whom do you give the pot?”, and _nu kevu stalu trum hauvu?_ asks “From where do you come?”. _Nu koda ushta su?_ asks “Do you have a pot?”, with _su_ after the complete utterance. After unclear speech, the fixed call _kevu?_ asks for it to be repeated or clarified.

The quantity comparisons use _barnu_, much or many. _Ehu koda uhu vrun bram barnu orsa_ means “These pots are more numerous than those”; _ehu jalka uhu vrun bram barnu orsa_ means “This water is greater in amount than that”. The reference _ehu koda_ points to the pots being counted; _ehu jalka_ points to the water being measured. _Nerm barnu_ gives fewer pots or less water. _Ehu jalka sartu navu vrun bram barnu orsa_ means “This is the most water”, more in amount than every other portion being compared; with _nyem_, it is the least. _Samrul_ gives exactness, _shemru_ approximation, and _nardun_ almost reaching the stated amount.

_Vre_ affirms a claim or question; _nuk_ rejects it. _Sotyak_ means true, while _sotyanir_ means personally convinced. A mistaken person can be _sotyanir_ about something that is _uvas sotyak_, false. _Bosha_ is an intentional lie; _sotyabrak_ also includes an innocent false claim. _Dirmul_ reports what appearances suggest, _lamprul_ what an unconfirmed report says, _grendu_ what is probable, and _dvirnul_ a possibility to which the speaker is not committed. _Sotyal_ asserts sincerity; _sotya bhe_ asserts the actual facts.

_Mo nu uvas samrak_ means “I disagree with you”, withholding assent to the view being discussed. _Mo dvirnuk_ is “I doubt it”; _mo vredun_, “I suspect it”; _mo gansuk_, “I am guessing”; and _mo sotruk_, “I assume it”. The claim in question is supplied by the conversation in these short utterances.

_Borsha narta, drunva mo groha bhe nyuru orsa_ means “It rains; therefore I stay at home”. _Mo groha bhe nyuru orsa drunem borsha narta_ means “I stay at home because it rains”. _Vren borsha narta, anven mo groha bhe nyuru orsa_ means “If it rains, then I stay at home”. For “I go unless it rains”, the condition is negated: _vren borsha uvas narta, anven mo chambu_, “If rain does not happen, then I go”. This states what follows when there is no rain. _Vren borsha narta, anven mo groha bhe nyuru orsa; navren mo chambu_ means “If it rains I stay at home; otherwise I go”.

_Nervren borsha narta, mo chambu_ means “Although it rains, I go”; _borsha nervru mo chambu_ is “Despite the rain I go”. _Borsha narta; nerul mo chambu_ says “It rains; however, I go”, and _nyurul_ in place of _nerul_ gives “nevertheless”. _Mo jalka jalnu; nu navul ghomra ghuma_ means “I drink water; you eat food instead”.

_Vau nu ghuma vau nu jalnu_ offers “Either you eat or you drink”; the two _vau_ mark the alternatives. _Nukva mo ghuma nukva mo jalnu_ means “Neither do I eat nor do I drink”; each _nukva_ excludes its clause. _Mo uvas ghuma nukva mo jalnu_ continues a negative claim with “nor do I drink”. _Le mo ghuma le mo jalnu_ includes both: “I both eat and drink”. _Mo vren nu ghuma vau nu jalnu uvas jnenuk_ means “I do not know whether you eat or drink”; the alternatives stand before the main _uvas jnenuk_, “do not know”.

_Ehu uhu shem samrek orsa_ means “This is like that”; _ehu uhu shem uvas samrek orsa_ means “This is unlike that”. _Mo uhu ro samnu pantra shem ganu_ means “I count as that one does”, in the same way. _Mo shaltun lampu: nu atki orsa_ introduces a clarification: “That is: you are a guest”. _Mo tirma lampu: Darmu le Padmi_ gives the particular names intended: “Namely, Darmu and Padmi”. _Darsika shem, ehu koda_ introduces an example, “For example, this pot”.

_Sartu janru, nu nuvrek, mo shempal_ means “Everyone except you helps me”. _Mo aisul jalka jalnu_ means “I drink only water”; _mo levu ghomra ghuma_ adds “I also eat food”. _Mahvel nu mo shempal_ includes an unexpected helper: “Even you help me”. _Mo mokhyal nu shempal_ means “I especially help you”.

_Ehu mahrul drau orsa_ means “This is very long”. _Mahrul mahrul_ intensifies it to “extremely”; _kerul_ moderates it to “somewhat”, and _letrul_ gives “slightly”. _Ehu jalka nardubram barnu orsa_ means “This is too much water”, more than is sufficient or acceptable; _nardunyem barnu_ means too little. _Mo nardul grenu bhornu_ means “I can barely carry it”, only just meeting the relevant ability threshold.

_Mo kavra grenu bhornu_ means “I can carry the shield”, an ability; _mo kavra uvas grenu bhornu_ means “I cannot carry the shield”, lacking that ability. _Mo dvarna vranu muku_ means “I may open the door”, having permission. _Mo dvarna nyak uvas muku_ means “I must not open the door”, a prohibition. _Mo dvarna nyak brondu_ requires me to close the door; _mo dvarna vardun brondu_ advises that I ought to close it. _Borsha grenem narta_ means “Rain might happen”, a possibility rather than permission.

_Mo nu dvarna muku vrannuk_ means “I permit you to open the door”; _mo nu dvarna muku vranbrak_ means “I prohibit you from opening the door”. _Mo kamvin orsa_ expresses willingness, while _mo uvas kamvin orsa_ expresses unwillingness. _Mo mukru orsa_ means “I am free to act”, unconstrained; it does not merely grant permission for one act. _Ehu karmu karmunin orsa_ describes this act as intended, and _ehu karmu karmuvra orsa_ describes it as accidental.

The formal greeting _Agarana dulipati, vandikana_ is kept as the whole formula of respect to an elder given below. The friendly _nu kalnu orsa_ wishes the hearer health, on meeting or parting. _Nu atki orsa_ welcomes someone as a guest. These are fixed calls in their own right. So are the addressed requests _nu nyura bhinu_ “Listen”, _nu darmu_ “Look”, _nu nirpak_ “Wait”, _nu mo shempal_ “Help me”, _nu brondu_ “Stop”, and _nu mo vimbhu_ “Forgive me”. Their words can be used directly in these calls.

_Nimbra shem_ marks a polite request, “Please”. _Nimbra shem, mo jahnuk_ asks for attention, “Excuse me; I ask”. After a favour, _mo pratnu lampu_ is the addressed thanks; _pratnu uvas nyakun orsa_ replies that thanks are not required. _Mo shardun ushta_ expresses regret, “I am sorry”. _Nu ehu stalu tral hauvu_ asks someone to come here, and _nu durul chambu_ asks them to go away.

_Mo jnenru_ says “I understand”; _mo uvas jnenru_, “I do not understand”. _Nu ro shaltun kevu orsa?_ asks “What do you mean?”. _Nu ehu vedyari bhe kevu pantra shem lampu?_ asks how to say this in Vedyari; _ehu ro tirma kevu orsa?_ asks what it is called. _Ehu sotyak orsa su?_ asks “Really?”, seeking confirmation. _Kevu?_ requests repetition or clarification; _vramul!_ requests it again. _Kartul uvas_ gives the complete negative response “Not at all”.

_Kulpa_ keeps its Vedyari meaning, kin of a lineage within a station. _Janrakulpa_ names a tribe in the ordinary sense of a people organized by kinship; _janrasangra_ a community joined by place or way of life. _Karmu ro kuva_ is a worker generally, distinct from the hired _karmakuva_. _Samrudorma_ is justice, fair treatment and fair application of law; _dormin_ is moral rightness. _Mo ro varsu_ names my property, while _ronyur_ names ownership itself. _Ormona_ is life, the activity of living, and _ormoka_ a living being, built from the existing bound _orm-_ with the documented _-ona_ and _-oka_. _Kartuma_ is completion, distinct from a pleasant feeling of satisfaction; _navronda_ is transformation, a profound change of form or nature.

Worthlessness is expressed as _shon orgha ro_, “of zero worth”: _ehu shon orgha ro orsa_ judges this thing to have no worth in the valuation being made. A thing with modest but real value is not thereby worthless; _uvas orghin_ alone would only deny substantial worth.

## Script & Literacy

**Classical Script:** Vedyari employs the [[skill-vdykshrscrpt|Vedyakshara]], a syllabic script of approximately 48 distinct characters, each representing a CV syllable. Consonant clusters and final consonants are marked through diacritical modification of the base glyph. The script evolved from an older abjadic writing system (now preserved only in sacred contexts) and shows clear influence from neighboring systems.

**Common Cursive:** A more flowing, connected variant has developed for administrative and mercantile use, reducing the syllabic inventory to roughly 36 core forms with additional ligatures.

**Literacy:** Formal education in Classical Vedyari is the province of the priesthood, professional scribes, and about one noble in six. Even in the wealthiest city-states, the general population is overwhelmingly non-literate; merchants and guildsmen possess functional literacy at best—enough to recognize trade marks and numerals—and rely on scribes for contracts and record-keeping. In hinterland regions, literacy is virtually nonexistent outside the local temple. The mercantile caste has driven adoption of simplified script variants, but these are used by hired scribes rather than merchants themselves.

## Historical Development

### Proto-Vedyari

Vedyari's roots lie in an isolate family with no clear external relations, on the evidence of the archaic ritual texts and the etymologies. Some scholars propose remote connections to the tongues of the far east (_[[skill-tantsoulng|Tantsou Language]]_) and the southwest (_[[skill-khelathlng|Khelathi Language]]_). Nobody has shown one.

### Classical Period (Age of Kingdoms)

The standardization of Classical Vedyari occurred roughly 1,200 years before the present, during the reign of the philosopher-kings of [[place-madustan|Madustan]]. This period saw the composition of the great philosophical commentaries, legal codes, and epic narratives that remain the prestige texts of the civilization.

### Modern Era

Since [[lore-stndrdmdstn|the Standardization at Madustan]], gradual sound changes have created divergence:

Lenition of intervocalic stops
: p, t and k soften between vowels in some dialects (more pronounced in southern city-states)

Nasalization of vowels before nasals
: Incipient; most evident in rapid speech

Classical Vedyari remains largely frozen in literature and formal address, but vernacular speech has simplified many inflectional endings and leveled irregular paradigms.

## Regional Dialects

### Northern Vedyarian (Varcine Region)

- Retains more conservative pronunciation
- Preserves older endings
- Stress patterns follow classical norms (the second-to-last syllable receives primary stress)

### Southern Vedyarian (Coastlands & Trading Cities)

- More rapid speech; intervocalic lenition more pronounced
- Inflectional endings frequently reduced to single vowels
- Stress shift: increasing tendency toward word-initial stress in compounds
- Greater borrowing from neighboring linguistic systems

### Eastern Outlier Dialect (Remote Merchant Colonies)

- Extreme simplification of agreement
- Loss of gender distinctions in some contexts
- Extensive code-switching with neighboring trade languages
- Represents early stages of creolization

## Sample Phrases

Agarana dulipati, vandikana
: _Reverential greeting; "Honored elder, I show respect"_

Vedyaram tivayu, tarajisum
: _Formal statement of origin; "I dwell in Vedyaram, by birthright"_

Bhaktir ushtami dulitara
: _Expression of devotion; "I hold devotion as my highest path"_

Vastuna karmata vanisa?
: _Mercantile inquiry; "May the transaction bring prosperity?"_

Rituta samarvana ushti
: _Ritual formula; "The observance is offered to the sacred order"_

## Related Languages

While Vedyari remains an isolate, linguistic diffusion from neighboring regions has created a zone of areal features:

Khelathi
: Some scholars note possible loan words in the domains of ritual and royal administration, though the borrowing direction remains disputed

[[skill-okharclng|Okharik Language]]
: Limited mutual intelligibility; some lexical similarities in trade vocabulary, possibly mediated through intermediary contact

Minor Trade Linguas
: Vedyari speakers have influenced and been influenced by simplified pidgins used in maritime commerce, particularly in the harbors of southern city-states

## Naming Traditions

### Cultural Significance

Vedyari names are cosmologically and philosophically charged. A personal name states the family lineage, the spiritual aspiration and the social position of the person who carries it. Names commonly take elements from mythology, from virtue terms or from the celestial bodies, and bind the individual to the metaphysical order.

### Naming Patterns

**Given Names (Male):**

- Often compounds of two or three words, joining a virtue, a god's favor or a quality of the sun or the moon to a word for what the bearer is to be
- Frequent elements: _palda_ (keeper), _pavi_ (lord), _rajva_ (king), _-deva_ (of _devra_, a god), _birda_ (hero), _vikroma_ (valor), _anonda_ (joy), _kirta_ (fame)
- Preferred vowel harmony creates euphonious, rhythmic quality
- Examples: _Darmapalda_ (keeper of the law), _Senapavi_ (lord of the host), _Shilabir_ (hero of the stone), _Madurava_ (sweet voice)

**Given Names (Female):**

- Similarly compound, often incorporating elements denoting grace, devotion, or cosmic principle
- Common endings: _-i_, _-ika_ and _-a_, which close the names of both women and men
- Deity names and sacred geographical features appear frequently
- Examples: _Padmavali_ (a row of lotuses), _Chandreli_ (moon, the beautiful), _Vanika_ (of the grove)

**Clan Names:**

- A compound of two words, sometimes three
- Often feature ancient founder names or mythological ancestral figures
- May include place or occupational elements
- Examples: _Danurkirti_ (bow-fame), _Vidsamondal_ (the circle of learning), _Tejanand_ (brilliance and joy)

### Honorifics and Titles

Formal address often includes patronymic constructions (_-suta_, _-vaya_: "offspring of") and epithetive titles denoting virtue, rank, or accomplishment. These may be appended or prefixed, creating elaborate formal names used in ceremonial and official contexts.

### Calling Names

Beside the formal name, every Vedyari has a _calling name_, a short form of the given name used by kin and friends and offered rather than taken: _Padmavali_ is _Padmi_ at home. It keeps the first one or two syllables of the given name and closes on _-u_, _-i_ or _-a_, or doubles the first syllable. The lexicon named below gives the rule in full, with the calling names the setting records.

## Lexicon

The words of Vedyari, the rules a word is built by and a register of every Vedyari name the setting uses stand in the [[skill-vedyarlng#lexicon|Vedyari Lexicon]].

## Vedyari Lexicon {#lexicon}

A temple scribe in [[place-rajepur|Rajepur]] who teaches a newcomer to read the names on a map begins with the doors. _Dvarna_ is a door, and the passes through [[place-sthrnwall|the Southern Wall]] are doors: [[place-suryadvar|Suryadvar]] the sun's door, [[place-meghadvar|Meghadvar]] the storm's, [[place-tamradvar|Tamradvar]] the copper road's. Learn twenty such words and the map of [[place-vedyarargn|Vedyara]] reads itself, because almost every Vedyari name is two or three ordinary words set side by side.

This is the word-hoard of the Vedyari tongue: the words a player or a GM reaches for when a Vedyari names a child, a village, an office or a god, the rules those words obey, and the way two of them join into a third. A word made from these by those rules is a Vedyari word. The last section is a register of every Vedyari name the setting uses, with the note where each is met and the words it is made from.

Every word here is written as names are written across the setting.

## How to read the dictionary {#how-to-read-the-dictionary}

An entry gives a noun in its plain form. A verb citation ending in a hyphen is a bound stem and never stands alone; separately listed spoken verbs are free words, given with their ordinary use. A word made of smaller parts names the words and suffixes the form is made of, joined by `+`, and the form is exactly what the rules below make of them.

### Fields

```sql
SELECT name AS "Field", holds AS "What it holds"
FROM fields
WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng'
ORDER BY position
```

## Sounds and spelling {#sounds-and-spelling}

Its words are written in the setting's one guide; see [[doc-prnncthlrn|Pronouncing Thalorna]].

### Word edges

A word opens on a vowel, on a single consonant or on one of the pairs below, and closes on a vowel or on one of the consonants below. A verb stem is bound and never closes a word, so it may end on any consonant.

```sql
SELECT CASE edge WHEN 'openCluster' THEN 'Pairs that may open a word' WHEN 'closeConsonant' THEN 'Consonants that close a word' END AS "Edge", coalesce(nullif(array_to_string(list_transform(sounds, x -> '`' || x || '`'), ', '), ''), '—') AS "Sounds"
FROM edges WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' ORDER BY position
```

### Clusters

```sql
SELECT 'Most consonants together inside a word' AS "Rule", max AS "Count"
FROM clusters WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng'
```

Two vowels never stand side by side except as `ai` and `au`; where two words would bring them together, the joining below decides what is written.

## Building words {#building-words}

Two moves make every built word in this lexicon, and a new word made the same way is as good a Vedyari word as any here.

**A compound.** Two words set side by side make one, and the first describes the second: _surta_ "the sun" and _dvarna_ "a door" give _suryadvar_, the sun's door. Most names of places and offices are compounds, and many have three or four members: _meghra_, _mukhva_ and _kalda_ give _meghamukhakala_, the month of the mouth of the clouds. A hyphen may stand at a seam, and where it does the two words keep their own shapes: _Surta-samedra_.

**A suffix.** A word or a verb stem takes one of the suffixes below: _gan-_ "count" takes _-oka_ to give _ganoka_, a reckoner, and _vreta_ "a vow" takes _-in_ to give _vretin_, one under a vow.

### Joining

Where the first word of a compound closes on a vowel and the second opens on one, the two vowels meet as the table says. Every other seam writes both words whole.

```sql
SELECT array_to_string(list_transform(first, x -> '`' || x || '`'), ', ') AS "First closes on", array_to_string(list_transform(second, x -> '`' || x || '`'), ', ') AS "Second opens on",
       '`' || written || '`' AS "Written", coalesce(example, '—') AS "Example"
FROM joining WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' ORDER BY position
```

### Suffixes

```sql
SELECT '`' || form || '`' AS "Suffix", attaches AS "Stem", gloss AS "Makes", coalesce(nullif(array_to_string(list_transform(examples, e -> regexp_replace(e, '^(\S+) > ([^\s,;]+)', '_\1_ > _\2_')), ', '), ''), '—') AS "Example"
FROM affixes
WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng'
ORDER BY position
```

## Words {#words}

### Sun, moon, sky and the reckoning of time

```sql
SELECT form AS "Form", class AS "Class", gloss AS "Gloss", built_text AS "Built from", coalesce(array_to_string(list_transform(attested, a -> '[[' || a || '|]]'), ', '), '—') AS "Attested"
FROM words
WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' AND field = 'Sun, moon, sky and the reckoning of time' AND split_part(class, ' ', 1) IN ('name', 'stem', 'pref')
ORDER BY position
```

### Water, rain and the monsoon

```sql
SELECT form AS "Form", class AS "Class", gloss AS "Gloss", built_text AS "Built from", coalesce(array_to_string(list_transform(attested, a -> '[[' || a || '|]]'), ', '), '—') AS "Attested"
FROM words
WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' AND field = 'Water, rain and the monsoon' AND split_part(class, ' ', 1) IN ('name', 'stem', 'pref')
ORDER BY position
```

### Mountain, forest, field and the shapes of the land

```sql
SELECT form AS "Form", class AS "Class", gloss AS "Gloss", built_text AS "Built from", coalesce(array_to_string(list_transform(attested, a -> '[[' || a || '|]]'), ', '), '—') AS "Attested"
FROM words
WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' AND field = 'Mountain, forest, field and the shapes of the land' AND split_part(class, ' ', 1) IN ('name', 'stem', 'pref')
ORDER BY position
```

### Gods, rites and the temple

```sql
SELECT form AS "Form", class AS "Class", gloss AS "Gloss", built_text AS "Built from", coalesce(array_to_string(list_transform(attested, a -> '[[' || a || '|]]'), ', '), '—') AS "Attested"
FROM words
WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' AND field = 'Gods, rites and the temple' AND split_part(class, ' ', 1) IN ('name', 'stem', 'pref')
ORDER BY position
```

## Making names {#making-names}

**A person** carries a given name and the name of a clan, the given name first: [[being-nrvtjnnd|Narava Tejanand]] is Narava of the clan of brilliance and joy. A given name is one word or a compound of two or three, wishing something on the child: a virtue, a god's favor, a quality of the sun or the moon. _Chandreli_ is "moon, the beautiful."

### Given names

```sql
SELECT string_agg('`' || form || '`', ', ' ORDER BY position) AS "Ending", gloss AS "Given to"
FROM affixes WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' AND kind = 'name'
GROUP BY gloss ORDER BY min(position)
```

A woman's name is often a man's word with _-i_ in place of its closing _a_: _sundora_ "beautiful" gives _sundeli_.

**A clan** is named by a compound of two words, sometimes three, which keeps the founder's name or the virtue the lineage claims: _Danurkirti_ is bow-fame, and _Vidsamondal_ is the circle of learning. A clan name does not change with the gender of the person who carries it.

**A place** is a compound whose last member says what kind of place it is, and whose first member says which:

```sql
WITH named AS (
  SELECT alias AS label, min(slug) AS slug FROM (SELECT unnest(name.aliases) AS alias, address.slug AS slug FROM notes WHERE type = 'place') GROUP BY alias
  UNION ALL
  SELECT name.full, address.slug FROM notes WHERE type = 'place' AND name.full NOT IN (SELECT unnest(name.aliases) FROM notes WHERE type = 'place')
)
SELECT '`' || e.form || '`' AS "Last member", e.sense AS "A place that is",
       '[[' || n.slug || '|' || split_part(e.examples[1], ', ', 1) || ']]' || substring(e.examples[1], length(split_part(e.examples[1], ', ', 1)) + 1) AS "Example"
FROM elements e JOIN named n ON lower(n.label) = lower(split_part(e.examples[1], ', ', 1))
WHERE e.language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' AND e.kind = 'place' ORDER BY e.position
```

A person from a place takes _-i_: a [[place-rajepur|Rajepur]] man is a Rajepuri, a woman of [[affiliation-vyalendra2|Vyalendra]] a Vyalendri.

**An office** is a compound whose last member says what the holder does: _palda_ keeps (_shetrapalda_, a keeper of fields), _pavi_ presides (_sabhapavi_, the presiding officer of a sabhra), _nayeka_ leads, _adyoksha_ oversees, _achorya_ teaches. A title from a verb takes _-oka_, as _dorshaka_, a seer, or _-in_, as _vretin_, one under a vow. The great titles are compounds with _mahva_: _maharava_, _mahaprasoda_.

**A god** of the [[affiliation-vrnkpnthn|Varnuka]] is named by a compound that says what the god keeps or does: [[lore-kalavratadty|Kalavrata]] is the vow of death, [[lore-meghonatadty|Meghonata]] the lord of the clouds, [[lore-vyalendradty|Vyalendra]] the chief of beasts. A god's adjective takes _-an_, and a god's day takes _vura_.

**A work** of literature or law is named in the reader's tongue by what it does, as The [[lore-rcitkngsmhnd|Recitation of the Kings of Mahanodi]] is.

### Calling names

A woman entered on the temple roll as _Padmavali_ answers to _Padmi_ in her mother's kitchen. The long name is the formal one: it stands in records, in the temple and before a court, and it is the name a stranger uses. The short one is the _calling name_, used by kin and friends, and it is offered, never taken; the [[lore-vedyariclt|Vedyari]] culture note says what follows from that. Every calling name is cut from the given name by one rule, and it is never made from the clan name.

**Counting syllables.** A syllable is counted by its vowel. Each vowel and diphthong is one syllable, so _Pritimaja_ has four: _pri·ti·ma·ja_. A single consonant between two vowels opens the second syllable; where several stand together, the first closes the syllable before and the rest open the next, as in _chan·dre·li_. A seam adds nothing of its own: where the joining has merged two vowels into one, as _podma_ and _avoli_ merge in _padmavali_, the merged vowel is a single syllable, _pad·ma·va·li_.

**Cutting the name.** Keep the first one or two syllables of the given name whole, carry over the consonants that open the next syllable, and close on `-u`, `-i` or `-a` where that syllable's vowel stood. Put another way, the given name is cut just before its second or third vowel and a closing is added: _Darmapalda_ is cut to _Darm-_ and gives _Darmu_, and _Padmavali_ is cut to _Padm-_ and gives _Padmi_. The letters before the cut are the given name's own, long vowels long; where a name is written with a stress mark the pages do not use, the vowel beneath it may be read short or long. The other way to make a calling name is to double the first syllable, writing its opening consonants and its vowel twice: _Suvarti_ gives _Susu_. A name that opens on a vowel has no doubled form, since two vowels never stand together.

A calling name is said aloud far more often than it is written, and _Pritimaja_ is cut to _Prit-_ and gives _Pritu_, and _Driksharana_ is cut to _Driksh-_ and gives _Driksha_. Apart from that, everything before the closing comes from the given name, and a calling name always closes on a vowel, so it keeps the word rules without effort.

```sql
SELECT coalesce('`' || form || '`', 'the first syllable doubled') AS "Closing",
       CASE usual WHEN 'man' THEN 'a man' WHEN 'woman' THEN 'a woman' ELSE usual END AS "Usual for"
FROM calling_names WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' ORDER BY position
```

A calling name in _-u_ is a man's and one in _-i_ a woman's, so _Darmu_ names a man and _Padmi_ a woman. The given names themselves are not divided this way: a short _-i_ closes a given name of either.

Every Vedyari has a calling name, and the pages record it wherever the given name is long. A person whose given name runs to the number of syllables below or more carries the calling name as an alias, and the register lists it in the tongue `calling`, with the given name it is cut from in its **Built from** cell.

```sql
SELECT 'Fewest syllables in a given name the pages give a calling name' AS "Rule", min_syllables AS "Count"
FROM calling_names WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' LIMIT 1
```

The calling names the pages record, each cut before the second vowel of the given name:

| Who                                            | Cut             | Calling name |
| ---------------------------------------------- | --------------- | ------------ |
| [[being-anrjhrshkr\|Anuraja Harshakar]]        | _An-_           | _Anva_       |
| [[being-chndrljyvl\|Chandreli Jayavalli]]      | _Chandr-_       | _Chandri_    |
| [[being-drmpldvmkt\|Darmapalda Vimuktan]]      | _Darm-_         | _Darmu_      |
| [[being-drikaranamn\|Driksharana Manrishti]]   | _Driksh-_       | _Driksha_    |
| [[being-madurvstrpl\|Madurava Sutrapal]]       | _Madr-_         | _Madru_      |
| [[being-pdmvldnrkrt\|Padmavali Danurkirti]]    | _Padm-_         | _Padmi_      |
| [[being-prtrkpdmdksh\|Pritarika Padmadiksha]]  | _Pri-_, doubled | _Pripri_     |
| [[being-prtmjkydr\|Pritimaja Kayadara]]        | _Prit-_         | _Pritu_      |
| [[being-senpvvkrmd\|Senapavi Vikramada]]       | _Sen-_          | _Senu_       |
| [[being-suvrtrtnsn\|Suvarti Ratnisen]]         | _Suv-_          | _Suvi_       |
| [[being-shlbrvdsmndl\|Shilabir Vidsamondal]]   | _Shil-_         | _Shilu_      |
| [[being-vrngdkshn\|Viranga Dukshana]]          | _Vir-_          | _Viru_       |
| [[being-vshvkhlrtndds\|Vishvakhila Ratnadasa]] | _Vishv-_        | _Vishvu_     |

## Attested names {#attested-names}

Every name the setting gives a Vedyaran person, place, god, office, school, month or thing, with every note where it is met. A name built from this lexicon shows how; a name the Vedyari use that is not yet built from it shows `—`. A name in the reader's tongue is listed so that the register is complete, and it is not built.

### Tongues

```sql
SELECT '`' || tag || '`' AS "Tongue", meaning AS "Meaning"
FROM tongues WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' ORDER BY position
```

### Names

```sql
SELECT '**' || name || '**' AS "Name",
       coalesce(array_to_string(list_transform(notes, n -> '[[' || n || '|' || name || ']]'), ', '), '—') AS "Notes",
       '`' || tongue || '`' AS "Tongue",
       coalesce(array_to_string(list_transform(built, x -> '`' || x || '`'), ' + '), '—') AS "Built from"
FROM register WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' ORDER BY position
```

### Retired names

Some names are kept out of the setting: the gods, persons, places and terms of Earth, and forms the rules refuse. Each stands below with the name the setting writes for the same thing, or a dash where it writes none, and no page writes them, however accented. A literature note quotes its work in the work's own words and a poem keeps its own, so a retired name may stand in either, and a `terran_analog` comment names the Earth source on purpose.

```sql
SELECT '`' || form || '`' AS "Retired",
       coalesce(array_to_string(list_transform(instead, x -> '`' || x || '`'), ', '), '—') AS "Written instead",
       why AS "Why"
FROM retired WHERE language = 'thalorna-note-skill-vedyarlng' ORDER BY position
```
